(b)(i) Write down the two’s complement representation of 1210 using 8 bits.
       1210 ixLHdfjys foflys wkqmQrlh (two’s complement) ìgq 8la Ndú;fhka ksrEmKh jk wdldrh
       ,shd olajkak'

     1210 is a positive number and will not pass through a NOT gate. Therefore, the two's complement of this
     is its binary value.
     1210 hkq Ok ixLHdjla ksid NOT oajdrhla yryd .uka lrkq fkd,efí' tu ksid fuys foflys
     wkqmQrlh jkafka fuys oaùuh w.huhs'

           2   12
           2   6    -   0
           2   3    -   0
           2   1    -   1
               0    -   1

     1210 in two’s complement (8 bits) = 00001100
     1210 foflys wkqmQrlfhka ^ìgq 8) = 00001100

 (ii) Write down the two’s complement representation of –6810 using 8 bits.
      – 6810 ixLHdfjys foflys wkqmQrlh ìgq 8la Ndú;fhka ksrEmKh jk wdldrh ,shd olajkak'

    First, convert the positive number of -68 into a binary number with 8 bits. (+68 in two’s complement)

     2   34   -   0
     2   17   -   0
     2   8    -   1
     2   4    -   0
     2   2    -   0
     2   1    -   0
         0    -   1

    6810 in two’s complement (8 bits) = 01000100
    6810 foflys wkqmQrlfhka ^ìgq 8) = 01000100

    Now pass the two's complement through a NOT gate. (-68 in one’s complement)
    oeka tu foflys wkqmQrlh NOT oajdrhla yryd .uka lrjkak' (-68 tflys wkqmQrlfhka)

     0 1 0 0 0 1 0 0 → NOT → 1 0 1 1 1 0 1 1

    Now add a binary 1 to the end of this number. / oeka fuu ixLHdfõ w.g oaùuh 1la tl;= lrkak'

      10111011
            +1
      10111100

    Now, this is the two’s complement of -6810 . Therefore, -68 in two’s complement = 101111002.
    oeka" fuh -68 ys foflys wkqmQrlh fõ' tuksid -68 ys foflys wkqmQrlh = 101111002.

 (iii) Compute –6810 + 1210 using the above representations (i) and (ii).
       by; (i) yd (ii) ys ksrEmKh Ndú;fhka – 6810 + 1210 .Kkh lrkak'

       00001100
      +10111100
       11001000

    wkqmQrlfhks'

    Since we know very well that the answer obtained by subtracting -6810 +1210 is a negative number, we
    can say that the two's complement number obtained is a negative number. This can be obtained by
    reversing the process and converting it to a decimal number.

 (iv) State one advantage of using two’s complement representation for data in internal operations of a
      computer.
      mß.Klhl wNHka;r fufyhqï i|yd o;a;" foflys wkqmQrlh f,i ksrEmKh lsÍfuka ,efnk tla
      jdishla i|yka lrkak'

    1. Simplifies Arithmetic Operations / .Ks;uh l%shdj,s ir, lsÍu'
    •   Addition, subtraction, and multiplication can be performed using the same hardware for both signed
        and unsigned numbers, reducing complexity.
        ,l=Kq iys; yd ,l=Kq rys; ixLHd i|yd tlu oDvdx.hla Ndú;fhka tl;= lsÍu" wvq lsÍu iy
        .=K lsÍu isÿ l< yels neúka ixlS¾K;dj wvq fõ'

    2. No Separate Hardware for Signed & Unsigned Operations
       ,l=Kq iys;$rys; l%shdj,s i|yd fjku oDvdx. wjYH ke;

    •   The same ALU (Arithmetic Logic Unit) can handle both signed (two's complement) and unsigned
        numbers without modification.
        tlu ALU (Arithmetic Logic Unit) tAllhla u.ska ,l=Kq iys; iy ,l=Kq rys; ixLHd fjkia
        lsÍï fkdue;sj ielish yelsh'

    3. Efficient Subtraction Using Addition / tl;= lsÍu Ndú;fhka wvq lsÍfï ld¾hlaIu;dj'

    •   Subtraction can be performed by simply adding the two's complement of the subtrahend, eliminating
        the need for a separate subtraction circuit.
        wvq l< hq;= ixLHdfõ fofla wkqmQrlh tl;= lsÍu u.ska wvq lsÍu isÿ l< yels neúka fjku
        wvq lsÍfï mßm:hla wjYH fkdfõ'

    4. Single Zero Representation / Y=kHfha ;ks ksrEmKh'

    •   Unlike one's complement (which has +0 and -0), two's complement has only one representation for
        zero, avoiding ambiguity.
        tfla wkqmQrl l%uhg (+0 iy -0 f,i) idfmalaIj fofla wkqmQrl l%ufha Y=kHhg tla
        ksrEmKhla muKla we;s neúka jHdl+,;d wju fõ'

    5. Natural Overflow Handling / iajdNdúl w;sm%jdy (overflow) yeisrùu

    •   Overflow in two's complement arithmetic is easily detected by checking if the carry into the sign bit
        differs from the carry out.
        ,l=Kq iys; ìgqjg we;=¿ jk iy msgjk carry w.hka ixikaokh lsÍfuka fofla wkqmQrl
        .Ks;fha w;sm%jdyh myiqfjka y÷kd .; yelsh'

    6. Consistent Sign Extension / ia:djr ,l=Kq §¾>lrKh (sign extension)

    •   Extending a number to more bits (e.g., 8-bit to 16-bit) is simple: just replicate the sign bit (sign
        extension).
        ìgq m%udKh jeä lsÍfï§ ,l=Kq iys; ìgqj muKla mqkrdj¾;kh lsÍu u.ska ir,j §¾>lrKh
        l< yelsh'

    7. Symmetrical Range for Negative & Positive Numbers
       RK iy Ok ixLHd i|yd iuñ;sl mrdih

    •   The range is slightly asymmetrical but more balanced than sign-magnitude (e.g., -128 to +127 for 8-
        bit), allowing efficient use of bits.
        ìgq 8 i|yd -128 isg +127 olajd jQ wiuñ;sl mrdihla ;snqKo" th ,l=Kq iys; ksrEmK
        l%uhg jvd iu;=,s; jk w;r ìgq iM,;djfhka Ndú;hg yelshdj ,nd fohs'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                               -72-                                          #ictfromabc

     8. Easier Comparison of Signed Numbers / ,l=Kq iys; ixLHd ixikaokh lsÍu myiqh

     •   Comparing two's complement numbers (for greater/less than) can be done using the same logic as
         unsigned numbers after adjusting for the sign bit.
         ,l=Kq iys; ìgqj ilia lsÍfuka miqj" fofla wkqmQrl ixLHd ,l=Kq rys; ixLHd fukau
         ixikaokh l< yelsh'

     9. Simplifies Negative Number Detection / RK ixLHd y÷kd .ekSu ir,hs

     •   The most significant bit (MSB) acts as a sign flag (1 for negative, 0 for positive), making sign
         checks straightforward.
         jeäu fjfiis ìgqj ^MSB& ,l=Kq ixfla;hla f,i l%shd lrhs ^1 } RK" 0 } Ok&" tuksid
         ,l=Kq mÍlaId lsÍu iDcqju isÿ l< yelsh'

     10. Widely Adopted Standard / m%p,s; iïu; l%uh

     •   Since most processors use two's complement, software compatibility and optimization are improved
         across different systems.
         fndfyda ilikhka fofla wkqmQrl l%uh Ndú;d lrk neúka" úúO moaO;s w;r uDÿldx.
         wkql+,;dj iy m%Yia;lrKh jeäÈhqKq ù we;'

(b)(i) Identify and explain the fundamental error in the following Data Flow Diagram (DFD).
       my; oelafjk o;a; .e,Sï igyfkys (DFD) we;s uQ,sl fodaIhla y÷kdf.k meyeÈ,s lrkak'

                    1.0                        2.0

          pay                          pay                        cheque

             Allowances and Deductions                 Salary

     other in the diagram without going through a process. In DFD rules, data should never flow directly
     between two data stores.

     Statement / m%ldYkh 1
     True. A software agent works autonomously. It can perform tasks on its own to reach a goal, without
     needing someone to always tell it what to do.
     i;H fõ' uDÿldx. ksfhdað;fhla iajdëkj l%shd lrhs' b,lalhla lrd <Õd ùug thg ;ksju

     Statement / m%ldYkh 2
     True. Applications (like databases, web services) can communicate and exchange data with software
     agents automatically, without needing the user to be involved every time.
     i;H fõ' fhÿï ^o;a; iuqodhka" fjí fiajd jeks& uDÿldx. ksfhdað;hka iuÕ iajhxl%Shj

    Interrupt / w;=reì÷u
    An interrupt is a signal from hardware or software indicating that an event needs immediate attention.
    w;=reì÷ula hkq jevigyklg laIKsl wjOdkhla wjYH nj fmkakqï lrk oDvdx. fyda uDÿldx.
    j,ska ,efnk ix{djls'
    When an interrupt occurs, the OS might switch the currently running process to a different state to
    handle the interrupt.In the diagram the running process has being switched to the ready state.
    ndOdjla we;s jQ úg" fufyhqï moaO;sh uÕska ndOdj yeisrùu i|yd oekg l%shd;aul jk l%shdj,sh
    fjk;a ;;ajhlg udre l< yelsh' rEm igyfka l%shd;aul jk l%shdj,sh iQodkï ;;ajhg udre lr
    we;

    Scheduler dispatch / kshulrKh u.ska f;dard heùu
    The scheduler is the OS component that decides which process gets to run on the CPU.
    kshulrKh hkq CPU u; l%shd;aul úh hq;= l%shdj,sh ;SrKh lrk OS ixrplhhs'
    Dispatch means the scheduler has chosen a process and is giving it CPU time.
    f;dard heùu hkafkka woyia lrkafka kshulrKh l%shdj,shla f;dardf.k thg CPU ld,h ,nd fok
    njhs'
    The schedular dispatch chooses a process from ready state and dispatch it to the running state.
    kshulrKh u.ska f;dard heùu iQodkï ;;ajfhka l%shdj,shla f;dard th Odjk ;;ajhg hjhs'

    I/O or event completion / wdodk$m%;sodk i|yd fyda isoaê i|yd ksu lsÍu
    A process might be waiting for input/output (like reading from a file or network) or for some other event
    to finish.
    l%shdj,shla wdodkh$m%;sodkh ^f.dkqjlska fyda cd,hlska lshùu jeks& fyda fjk;a isÿùula wjika jk
    f;la n,d isáh yel'
    Once that I/O or event is done, the process can move to a ready state.
    tu I/O fyda isÿùu isÿ jQ miq" l%shdj,sh iQodkï ;;ajhlg hd yel'

    I/O or event wait / wdodk$m%;sodk i|yd fyda isoaêhla i|yd n,d isàu
    A running process might need to perform I/O or wait for an event. When it does, it moves to a waiting
    state until that I/O or event is completed.
    l%shd;aul jk l%shdj,shlg I/O isÿ lsÍug fyda isoaêhla i|yd n,d isàug wjYH úh yelsh' th isÿ
    jQ úg" tu I/O fyda isÿùu iïmQ¾K jk f;la th /£ isàfï ;;ajhlg .uka lrhs'

    These answers accurately reflect the typical state transitions in a multitasking operating system.
    Processes move between states based on events like interrupts, I/O requests, and the scheduler's
    decisions.
    fuu ms<s;=re nyqld¾h fufyhqï moaO;shl idudkH ;;aj ixl%dka;s ksjerÈj ms<sìUq lrhs' w;=reì÷ï"
    wdodk$m%;sodk b,a,Sï iy kshulrKh u.ska f;dard heùu jeks isÿùï u; mokïj l%shdj,s ;;aj w;r
    .uka lrhs'

 (ii) For the process above, give one possible reason for the following transition trigger:
      by; l%shdj,shg wod<j my; ixl%dka;s l%shdrïNlhg ;=vq Èh yels tla fya;=jla fokak'

    interrupt / w;=re ì÷u

    OS decided to let another task run / fufyhqï moaO;sh ;j;a ld¾hhla l%shd;aul lsÍug bv §ug
    ;SrKh lsÍu'
    In modern operating systems, each running process gets only a limited time to use the CPU, called a
    time slice. When this time is up, the OS interrupts the running process, moves it back to the Ready state,
    and selects another process to run. This ensures that no single process keeps the CPU for too long,
    allowing fair sharing among multiple tasks.
    kùk fufyhqï moaO;sj," iEu Odjk l%shdj,shlgu CPU Ndú;d lsÍug ,efnkafka iSñ; ld,hla
    muKs" th ld, fldgila f,i ye¢kafõ' fuu ld,h wjika jQ úg" OS Odjk l%shdj,shg ndOd lrhs"
    th kej; iQodkï ;;a;ajhg f.k hhs" iy l%shd;aul ùug ;j;a l%shdj,shla f;dard .kS' fuh lsisÿ
    ;ks l%shdj,shla CPU È.= ld,hla ;nd fkd.kakd nj iy;sl lrhs" nyq ld¾hhka w;r idOdrKj
    fnod .ekSug bv i,ihs'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                              -82-                                        #ictfromabc

    Process timeout / l%shdj,sfhys ld,h ksuùu
    A process timeout happens when a process takes too long to complete an operation or response. If the
    allowed time limit (timeout period) is crossed, the OS or system will interrupt or stop the process to
    prevent it from blocking resources or delaying other tasks.
    l%shdj,shla fufyhqula fyda m%;spdrhla iïmQ¾K lsÍug jeä ld,hla .; jk úg l%shdj,s l,a
    bl=;aùula isÿ fõ' wjir ,;a ld, iSudj blaujd .shfyd;a" OS fyda moaO;sh iïm;a wjysr lsÍfuka
    fyda fjk;a ld¾hhka m%udo lsÍfuka je<elaùu i|yd l%shdj,shg ndOd lrhs fyda kj;ajkq we;'

    A higher priority process comes / by< m%uqL;djla we;s l%shdj,shla meñŒu
    When a higher priority process becomes ready to run, the operating system immediately interrupts the
    currently running process. This is called preemption. The OS moves the running process back to the
    Ready state and gives the CPU to the higher priority process. This ensures that important tasks (like
    system processes or urgent user requests) are handled quickly without delay.
    by< m%uqL;d l%shdj,shla l%shd;aul ùug iQodkï jQ úg" fufyhqï moaO;sh jydu l%shd;aul jk
    l%shdj,shg ndOd lrhs' fuh mQ¾j ks.uk f,i ye¢kafõ' OS l%shd;aul jk l%shdj,sh kej; iQodkï
    ;;a;ajhg f.k hk w;r CPU by< m%uqL;d l%shdj,shg ,nd fohs' fuh jeo.;a ld¾hhka ^moaO;s
    l%shdj,s fyda yÈis mßYS,l b,a,Sï jeks& m%udohlska f;drj blaukska yiqrejk nj iy;sl lrhs'

 (d) In a certain computer, the physical memory has a total capacity of 4GB. The size of a memory frame
    is 4KB.
    tla;rd mß.Klhl fN!;sl u;lfha (physical memory) uq¿ Odß;dj 4GB fõ' tu u;lfha rduqjl
    (frame) úYd,;ajh 4KB fõ'

    (i) Compute the total number of frames in the physical memory.
        fN!;sl u;lfha we;s uq¿ rduq ixLHdj .Kkh lr olajkak'

        4 × 1024 × 1024 / 4 frames (rduq)
        1048576 frames (rduq)
        210 × 210 frames (rduq)
        220 frames (rduq)

        Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu:

        We are asked to figure out how many frames are contained within the computer's total physical
        memory.
        mß.Klfha uq¿ fN!;sl u;lh ;=< rduq lShla wvx.= oehs fidhd .ekSug wfmka b,a,d isà'

        Physical Memory refers to the computer's RAM (Random Access Memory), the actual hardware that
        stores data temporarily while the computer is running.
        fN!;sl u;lh hkq mß.Klh l%shd;aul jk w;r;=r ;djld,slj o;a; .nvd lrk ienE
        oDvdx. jk mß.Klfha RAM (iiïNdù m%fõY u;lh) h'

        Gigabyte (GB): A unit of digital storage equal to 230 bytes (approximately 1 billion bytes).
        .s.dnhsÜ (GB): nhsÜ 230 (wdikak jYfhka nhsÜ ì,shk 1) g iudk äðg,a .nvd tAllhla'

        Kilobyte (KB): A unit of digital storage equal to 210 bytes (1024 bytes).
        lsf,danhsÜ (KB): nhsÜ 210 (nhsg 1024 ) g iudk äðg,a .nvd tAllhla'

        Memory Frame: A fixed-size block of physical memory.
        u;l rduqj( fN!;sl u;lfha ia:djr u;l fldgils'
        Operating systems divide memory into frames for efficient management.
        fufyhqï moaO;s ld¾hlaIu l<ukdlrKh i|yd u;lh rduq j,g fnohs'

        To find the number of frames, we need to / rduq .Kk fidhd .ekSug" wmg wjYH jkafka:

        First convert to a common unit / m<uqj fmdÿ tAllhlg mßj¾;kh lrkak'

       Since the total memory size is in GB and the size of a frame is in KB, we need to convert both to the
       same unit (KB) for accurate calculation.
       uq¿ u;l m%udKh GB j,ska iy rduqjl m%udKh KB j,ska jk neúka" ksjerÈ .Kkh lsÍu
       i|yd wms folu tlu tAllhlg KB mßj¾;kh l< hq;=h'

        4 GB = 4 × 1024 × 1024 KB
        4 KB = 4 KB

       Number of frames = Total physical memory size / Frame size
       rduq .Kk          = uq¿ fN!;sl u;l m%udKh $ rduqjl m%udKh
                         = (4 × 1024 × 1024 KB) / (4 KB)
       Total number of frames = 4 × 1024 × 1024 KB / 4 KB
       uq¿ rduq .Kk

       Alternative answers / úl,am ms<s;=re,

       1) 1048576 frames / rduq
       This answer can be obtained by executing the above calculation.(execution is not necessary)
       by; .Kkh lsÍu l%shd;aul lsÍfuka fuu ms<s;=r ,nd .; yel' (.Kkh lsÍu wjYH fkdfõ)

       2) 210 × 210 frames
       (4 × 1024 × 1024 KB) / (4 KB)
       1024 × 1024 KB = 210 × 210

       fuho (4 × 1024 × 1024 KB) / (4 KB) ir, lsÍfuka ,nd.;a ;j;a ms<s.; yels ms<s;=rls'

       So any of the above answers are correct / tneúka by; ms<s;=re j,ska ´kEu tlla ksjerÈ fõ'

    (ii) Compute the total number of frames in the physical memory.
         fufyhqï moaO;sh" mß.Klfha Odjkh jk tla tla l%shdj,shla (process) i|yd msgq j.=j (page

       Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu:

       What is a page table / msgq j.=jla hkq l=ulao?

       A page table is a data structure used in a computer's memory management system to map virtual
       addresses to physical addresses in a paging system.
       msgq j.=jla hkq mß.Klhl u;l l<ukdlrK moaO;shl w:;H ,smsk fN!;sl ,smsk j,g
       is;shï.; lsÍu i|yd Ndú;d lrk o;a; jHqyhls'

       It is a key component of virtual memory and allows processes to use more memory than physically
       available by translating virtual memory addresses into physical memory locations.
       th w:;H u;lfha m%Odk wx.hla jk w;r w:;H u;l ,smsk fN!;sl u;l msysgqï j,g
       mßj¾;kh lsÍfuka l%shdj,Skag fN!;slj ,nd .; yels m%udKhg jvd jeä u;lhla Ndú;d
       lsÍug bv i,ihs'

       Key Functions / m%Odk ld¾hhka
       • Address Translation: Maps virtual addresses to physical addresses.
          ,smsk mßj¾;kh( w:;H ,smsk fN!;sl ,smsk j,g is;shï.; lrhs'
       • Memory Protection: Ensures processes can only access their allocated memory.
          u;l wdrlaIdj( l%shdj,Skag tAjdfha fjka lr we;s u;lhg muKla m%fõY úh yels nj
          iy;sl lrhs'
       • Efficient Memory Utilization: Supports virtual memory, allowing processes to use more memory
          than available RAM.
          ld¾hlaIu u;l Ndú;h( l%shdj,Skag oekg mj;sk RAM u;lhg jvd jeä u;lhla Ndú;d
          lsÍug bv i,iñka w:;H u;lhg iyh olajhs

       The question is asking you to explain the purpose of the page table in operating systems, given that
       it maps process pages to memory frames.
       m%Yakh jkafka fufyhqï moaO;sj, msgq j.=fõ wruqK meyeÈ,s lsÍuhs" th l%shdj,s msgq u;l
       rduq j,g is;shï.; lrk nj § we;.

       Purpose of the Page Table / msgq j.=fõ wruqK:
       The page table is used for virtual memory management. Its primary purpose is to translate virtual
       addresses (used by a process) to physical addresses (in main memory).
       msgq j.=j w:;H u;l l<ukdlrKh i|yd Ndú;d lrhs' tys uQ,sl wruqK jkafka w:;H ,smsk
       (l%shdj,shla u.ska Ndú;d lrk) fN!;sl ,smsk (m%Odk u;lfha) njg mßj¾;kh lsÍuhs'

       Virtual Memory: Operating systems use virtual memory to allow processes to have a larger logical
       address space than the physical RAM available.
       w;:H u;lh( fufyhqï moaO;s w:;H u;lh Ndú;d lrñka l%shdj,Skag ,nd .; yels fN!;sl
       RAM u;l m%udKhg jvd úYd, ;d¾lsl ,smsk m%udKhla Ndú;d lsÍug bv i,id fohs'

       This means a process can think it has access to a contiguous block of memory, even if that memory
       is fragmented or partially stored on disk.
       fuhska woyia lrkafka l%shdj,shlg tu u;lh LKavkh ù fyda ;eáfha w¾O jYfhka .nvd
       lr ;snqK;a ;ukag hdno u;l fldgilg m%fõYh we;s nj is;Sug yels njhs'

       Paging: Virtual memory is typically implemented using a technique called paging.
       msgqlrKh( w:;H u;lh idudkHfhka l%shd;aul lrkq ,nkafka msgqlrKh kï ;dlaIKhla
       Ndú;d lrñks'

       The virtual address space is divided into fixed-size units called pages, and the physical memory is
       divided into units called frames.
       w:;H ,smsk wjldYh msgq f,i y÷kajk ia:djr m%udKfha tAllj,g fnod we;s w;r fN!;sl
       u;lh rduq f,i y÷kajk tAllj,g fnod we;'

       Role of a page table: The page table acts as a lookup table that the operating system uses to find the
       physical frame corresponding to a virtual page.
       msgq j.=jl ld¾hNdrh( msgq j.=j w:;H msgqjlg wkqrEm fN!;sl rduqj fidhd .ekSug fufyhqï
       moaO;sh Ndú;d lrk fijqï j.=jla f,i l%shd lrhs'

       When a process tries to access a memory location, the CPU uses the page table to translate the
       virtual address into the physical address in RAM.
       l%shdj,shla u;l msysgqulg m%fõY ùug W;aidy lrk úg" CPU tl msgq j.=j Ndú;d lr w:;H
       ,smskh RAM ys fN!;sl ,smskhg mßj¾;kh lrhs'

       Mapping Process Pages to Memory Frames / l%shdj,s msgq u;l rduq j,g wkqrEmKh:
       This part is essentially restating the core function of the page table.
       fuu fldgi uQ,sl jYfhka msgq j.=fõ uQ,sl ld¾hh kej; m%ldY lrhs'

       It emphasizes that the page table is responsible for maintaining the relationship between the logical
       pages used by a process and the actual physical frames in RAM where those pages are stored.
       l%shdj,shla Ndú;d lrk ;d¾lsl msgq iy tu msgq .nvd lr we;s RAM ys i;H fN!;sl rduq
       w;r iïnkaO;djh mj;ajd .ekSu i|yd msgq j.=j j.lsj hq;= nj th wjOdrKh lrhs'

       Why is this important / fuh jeo.;a jkafka wehs?

       Memory Protection: Page tables allow the operating system to control which parts of memory a
       process can access, preventing processes from interfering with each other.
       u;l wdrlaIdj( msgq j.= uÕska l%shdj,shlg m%fõY úh yels u;lfha fldgia md,kh lsÍug
       fufyhqï moaO;shg bv i,ihs" tuÕska l%shdj,Ska tlsfklg ndOd lsÍfuka j<lajhs'

       Efficient Memory Usage: Paging allows the operating system to load only the necessary portions of
       a process into memory, improving memory utilization.
       ld¾hlaIu u;l Ndú;h( msgqlrKh uÕska fufyhqï moaO;shg l%shdj,shl wjYH fldgia muKla
       u;lhg ,nd §ug bv i,ihs" tuÕska u;l Ndú;h ld¾hlaIu fõ

       Larger Virtual Address Space: Virtual memory allows processes to use more memory than is
       physically available.
       úYd, w:;H ,smsk wjldYh( w:;H u;lh l%shdj,Skag fN!;slj ,nd .; yels m%udKhg jvd
       jeä u;lhla Ndú;d lsÍug bv i,ihs'

       By using the page table to manage this mapping, the operating system provides a more efficient,
       secure, and flexible memory management system.
       fuu wkqrEmKh l<ukdlrKh lsÍu i|yd msgq j.=j Ndú;d lsÍfuka" fufyhqï moaO;sh jvd;a
       ld¾hlaIu" wdrlaIs; iy kuHYS,S u;l l<ukdlrK moaO;shla imhhs'

    (iii) With respect to the physical memory size, what is the benefit of using the technique of virtual
          memory in the above computer?
          fN!;sl u;lfha Odß;dj i,lk úg" by; mß.Klfha w;:HrEmS u;l (virtual memory)

       Explanation:
       This question is asking about the advantage of using virtual memory in a computer, especially when
       considering the limited size of the physical RAM (Random Access Memory).
       fuu m%Yakh mß.Klhl RAM ys iSñ; m%udKh ^iiïNdù m%fõY u;lh& i,ld n,k úg
       w:;H u;lh Ndú;d lsÍfï jdish .ek wihs'

       Physical Memory (RAM): This is the actual, physical memory chips in your computer. It's fast but
       limited    in   size.  In   this     case,    the    computer     has    4GB     of      RAM.
       fN!;sl u;lh (RAM) fuh Tfí mß.Klfha we;s ienE" fN!;sl u;l Ñm fõ' th fõ.j;a
       kuq;a m%udKfhka iSñ;h' fuu wjia:dfõ§" mß.Klfha 4GB RAM u;l m%udKhla we;'

       Virtual Memory: This is a technique where a portion of the hard drive is used as an extension of RAM.
       When the RAM is full, the operating system moves less frequently used data from RAM to the hard
       drive This allows you to run programs that require more memory than your physical RAM provides.
       w:;H u;lh( fuh oDV ;eáfha fldgila RAM ys È.=jla f,i Ndú;d lrk ;dlaIKhlss' RAM
       msÍ we;s úg" fufyhqï moaO;sh RAM ys isg oDV ;eáhg wvqfjka Ndú;d lrk o;a; f.k hhs'

       This allows you to run programs that require more memory than your physical RAM provides.
       fuh Tfí fN!;sl RAM imhk m%udKhg jvd jeä u;lhla wjYH jevigyka l%shd;aul lsÍug
       Tng bv i,ihs'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                            -86-                                        #ictfromabc

       The key benefit is that you can run programs that exceed the physical RAM capacity.
       m%Odk m%;s,dNh kï Tng fN!;sl RAM Odß;dj blaujd hk jevigyka l%shd;aul l< yels ùuhs'

       Without virtual memory, if you tried to run a program larger than 4GB on this computer, it would
       likely crash or run extremely slowly.
       w:;H u;lh fkdue;sj" Tn fuu mß.Klfha 4GB g jvd úYd, jevigykla l%shd;aul lsÍug
       W;aidy l<fyd;a" th ì| jeàug fyda w;sYhska fiñka l%shd;aul ùug bv we;'

       While virtual memory allows you to run larger programs, it's significantly slower than RAM. This is
       because accessing data on the hard drive is much slower than accessing data in RAM.
       w:;H u;lh Tng úYd, jevigyka l%shd;aul lsÍug bv i,ik w;r" th RAM tlg jvd
       ie,lsh hq;= f,i ukao.dó fõ' fuhg fya;=j oDV ;eáfha o;a; m%fõY ùu RAM ys o;a; m%fõY
       lsÍug jvd fnfyúka ukao.dó ùuhs'

       The operating system manages the movement of data between RAM and the hard drive, trying to keep
       the     most       frequently    used    data       in    RAM       for      faster      access.
       fufyhqï moaO;sh RAM iy oDV ;eáh w;r o;a; p,kh l<ukdlrKh lrhs" fõ.j;a m%fõYh

 2. Consider the following scenario.
    my; oelafjk ixisoaêh i,lkak'
     The XYZ company has six departments, namely Production, Accounts, Sales, Administration,
     Maintenance and Information Technology Services (IT). The following table shows the number of
     computers available in each of the departments.
     XYZ iud.ug ksIamdok" .sKqï" wf,ú" mßmd,k" kv;a;= iy f;dr;=re ;dlaIK fiajd kñka fomd¾;fïka;=
     yhla we;' tla tla fomd¾;fïka;=fjys we;s mß.Kl ixLHdj my; j.=fjys fmkajd we;'

      Department No            Department                                   Number of Computers
            D01                Production / ksIamdok                                 25
            D02                Accounts / .sKqï                                      30
            D03                Sales / wf,ú                                          18
            D04                Administration / mßmd,k                               30
            D05                Maintenance / kv;a;=                                  25
            D06                IT Services / f;dr;=re ;dlaIK fiajd                   28

     Each department needs to have their own local area network. Network administrator has received a class
     C IP address block 192.248.154.0/24. It is required to subnet the IP address block to satisfy the
     requirements of each department and allocate IP addresses to them.
     tla tla fomd¾;fïka;=jg ;ukaf.au ia:dkSh m%foaY cd,hla (LAN) wjYHj mj;S' cd, mßmd,l fj;
     C mka;sfha 192.248.154.0/24 IP ,smsk ldKavhla ,eî we;' iEu fomd¾;fïka;=jlu wjYH;d imqrd,ñka
     tla tla fomd¾;fïka;=jg IP ,smsk fjka flfrk wdldrhg" IP ,smsk ldKavh Wmcd,kh (subnet) lsÍug
     wjYHj we;'
  (i) How many addresses are available in the IP address block?
      IP ,smsk ldKavfhys fldmuK ,smsk ixLHdjla ;sfí o?
     Consider the given IP address.
     § we;s ,smskh i,luq'

     192.248.154.0/24

     The "/24" indicates that 24 bits are used for the network portion, leaving 32 - 24 = 8 bits for the host
     portion.
     "/24" uÕska cd, fldgi i|yd ìgq 24 la Ndú;d lrk nj;a" Odrl fldgi i|yd ìgq 32 - 24 = 8 la
     b;sß jk nj;a olajhs'

     Network bit / cd, ìgq -24
     Host bit / i;aldrl ìgq - 8 (32 – 24 = 8 )

     Therefore the number of available addresses is 28 = 256.
     tu ksid ,nd .; yels ,smsk .Kk 28 = 256 fõ'
 (ii) What are the first and the last addresses of the IP address block?
      IP ,smsk ldKavfhys m<uq iy wjidk ,smsk fudkjd o?
     Now let's find the first address and the last address.
     oeka m<uq fhduqj iy wjidk fhduqj fidhuq'

     When all host bits are set to 0, the first address is obtained and when all host bits are set to 1, the last
     address is obtained.
     i;aldrl ìgq ish,a, 0 l< úg m<uq fhduqj o, i;aldrl ìgq ish,a, 1 l< úg úg wjidk fhduqj
     o ,efí'

     First Address / m<uq fhduqj: 192.248.154.0
     Last Address / wjidk fhduqj: 192.248.154.255

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                               -92-                                          #ictfromabc

 (iii) How many host bits are required to create the required subnets?
       wjYH Wmcd, ks¾udKh lsÍu i|yd i;aldrl (host) ìgq fldmuK wjYH fõ o?

     The number of host bits required to create the number of subnets is calculated by the power of two
     equals the number of subnets required.
     Wmcd, .Kk ks¾udKh lsÍug wjYH i;aldrl ìgq .Kk .Kkh lr.kq ,nkafka wjYH Wmcd,
     .Kkg iudk jk foflys n,h u.sks'

     Accordingly, since it is necessary to create 6 subnets, the number of host bits required to create this
     subnet is 3 because the smallest power of 2 that is greater than or equal to 6 is third power of 2.
     tA wkqj" Wmcd, 6la ks¾udKh lr.ekSu wjYH jk neúka" fuu Wmcd,h ks¾udKh lsÍug wjYH
     i;aldrl ìgq .Kk 3 fõ" ukao 6g jvd jeä fyda iudk jk 2 ys l=vdu n,h 2 ys ;=kajk n,h
     jk neúks'

      “A software agent may or may not have a user interface”.
      “uDÿldx. tacka;jrhl=g mßYS,l w;=re uqyqK;la ;sîug fyda fkd;sîug fyda ms<sjk'”

      Do you agree with the above statement? State a reason referring to the above figure.
      by; m%ldYh yd Tn tlÕ jkafka o? rEmigyk wdY%fhka fya;=jla olajkak'

      From the given figure, we can see that Agent A interacts with both the User and the Application, meaning
      it could have a user interface for communication with the user. However, Agent B only communicates
      with Agent A and does not directly interact with the User or the Application. This suggests that Agent B
      operates without a user interface, functioning in the background to assist Agent A.
      § we;s rEmfhka" ksfhdað; A mßYS,lhd iy fhÿu hk folu iuÕ wka;¾ l%shd lrk nj wmg oel.;
      yelsh" tkï thg mßYS,lhd iuÕ ikaksfõokh i|yd mßYS,l w;=re uqyqK;la ;sìh yelsh' flfia

      l%shd;aul jk w;r ksfhdað; A g iydh ùu i|yd miqìfï l%shd lrk njhs'

      Thus, the figure supports the idea that some software agents may have a user interface (like Agent A),
      while others may not (like Agent B).

4. (a) Assume an input which contains a sequence of positive numbers. The sequence has at most 100 numbers.
       If the input sequence has n numbers where n < 100, then the end of the sequence is marked by making
       (n+1)th number –1.
       For e.g., the following input sequence has 8 positive numbers, where 9th input which is –1 marks the end.
       Ok ixLHd wkql%uhla we;s wdodkhla i,lkak' wkql%uhg Wmßu jYfhka ixLHd 100la ;sìh yels h'
       n < 100 f,i wkql%uhg ixLHd n ;sfí kï" (n+1) jk ixLHdj -1 f,i i,l=Kq lsÍfuka wkql%ufha
       wjidkh oelafõ'
       WodyrKhla f,i my; oelafjk wdodk wkql%uhg Ok ixLHd 8la we;s w;r 9 jk wdodkh jk -1

    (iii) Develop a Python program to implement the algorithm in the flowchart.
          .e,Sï igyfka we;s we,af.dß;uh l%shd;aul lsÍu i|yd mhs;ka fla;hla f.dvk.kak'

       There are many methods to implement the flowcharts functionality using Python. But the expected
       answer is as follows.
       mhs;ka Ndú;fhka .e,Sï igyka l%shdldß;ajh l%shd;aul lsÍug fndfyda l%u ;sfí' kuq;a
       wfmalaIs; ms<s;=r my; mßÈ fõ'

       # Using while loop - Expected answer
       tempL = input('Enter the list of numbers: ').split(',')
       L = [int(i) for i in tempL]
       K = int(input('Enter the value to be searched: '))
       n = len(L)

       i = 0
       while i < n:
          if L[i] == K:
                print('True')
                break
          i += 1
       if not i < n:
          print('False')

       In this method, the given list is split using commas (,) and stored in tempL variable. Then it rewrites
       the value as integers and stores them in L.
       fuu l%ufha§" § we;s ,ehsia;=j fldud ^"& Ndú;fhka split lr" tempL hk úp,Hhla ;=< .nvd
       lrkq ,efí' bkamiq th mQ¾K ixLHd f,i kej; ,shñka L ys .nvd lrhs'

       Then, the value that needs to be searched is requested as an input and stored after converting into an
       integer.
       bkamiq" fiúh hq;= w.h wdodkhla f,i b,a,d isák w;r mQ¾K ixLHdjla njg mßj¾;kh
       lsÍfuka miq .nvd lrkq ,efí'

       There’s a variable called 'n' to store the length of the list L.
       L ,ehsia;=fõ È. .nvd lsÍu i|yd 'n' kñka úp,Hhla ks¾udKh lr we;'

       i is the control variable of the while loop. while loop only works if i is lower than n. If i < n is true,
       then the if statement checks if L[i] is equal to K or not. If K is equal to L[i], it prints 'True' and stops.
       Else, i will increase by one and the loop will restart until there’s a match or the condition of the
       while loop becomes false.
       i hkq while Æmfha md,k úp,Hh fõ' while Æmh l%shd lrkafka i, n g jvd wvq kï muKs'
       i < n i;H kï" if m%ldYh L[i], K g iudko keoao hkak mÍlaId lrhs' K, L[i] g iudk kï"
       th 'True ' uqøKh lr k;r fõ' tfia fkdue;skï" i tllska jeä jk w;r while Æmfha
       .e,mSula fyda ;;a;ajh wi;H jk f;la Æmh kej; l%shd;aul fõ'

       If the while loop ends without finding a match, it means i is equal to n. So, second if statement
       becomes true when i is greater than or equal to n because the not it used before the condition.
       while Æmh" .e<mSula fidhd fkdf.k wjika jqjfyd;a" thska woyia jkafka i, n g iudk njhs'
       tneúka" fojk if m%ldYh i;H jkafka i, n g jvd úYd, fyda iudk jk úgh" ukao th
       fldkafoaishg fmr not Ndú; lr we;s neúks'

       It prints 'False' if there isn’t a match.
       .e<mSula fkdue;s kï th 'False' uqøKh lrhs'

       For extra knowledge, there are other methods to implement this and get the same output. But those

       wu;r oekqu i|yd" fuh l%shd;aul lsÍug iy tlu m%;sodkh ,nd .ekSug fjk;a l%u ;sfí'

       # Using while loop
       tempL = input('Enter the list of numbers: ').split(',')
       L = [int(i) for i in tempL]
       K = int(input('Enter the value to be searched: '))

       index = 0
       while index < len(L):
          if L[index] == K:
                print('True')
                break
          index += 1
       else:
          print('False')

       This method, the code use len(L) instead of creating a variable to store its value. After the while loop
       ends, there’s an else block instead of the if statement in above code. The else block works when the
       while loop becomes false so the functionality is same.
       fuu l%ufha§" fla;fha w.h .nvd lsÍu i|yd úp,Hhla ks¾udKh lsÍu fjkqjg len(L) Ndú;d

       # Using while loop - with found variable
       tempL = input('Enter the list of numbers: ').split(',')
       L = [int(i) for i in tempL]
       K = int(input('Enter the value to be searched: '))

       index = 0
       found = False
       while index < len(L):
          if L[index] == K:
                found = True
                break
          index += 1

       if found:
          print('True')
       else:
          print('False')

       In this code, there’s a variable named found. Initial value is False and the code will change its value

       code.

       # Using for loop
       tempL = input('Enter the list of numbers: ').split(',')
       L = [int(i) for i in tempL]
       K = int(input('Enter the value to be searched: '))

       for element in L:
          if element == K:
                print('True')
                break
       else:
          print('False')

       This is almost same; the only difference is there’s a for loop instead of the while loop in previous
       codes.
       fuho fndfyda ÿrg iudk fõ; tlu fjki jkafka fmr fla;j, ;snQ while Æmh fjkqjg fuys
       for Æmhla ;sîuhs'

       # Using for loop without break
       def search_in_list(L, K):
          for element in L:
                if element == K:
                      return True
          return False

       def main():
          tempL = input('Enter the list of numbers: ').split(',')
          L = [int(i) for i in tempL]
          K = int(input('Enter the value to be searched: '))
          print(search_in_list(L, K))

       main()

       In this code, there isn’t a break statement. Instead of break, this uses return to get the output values.
       main function has the codes to get inputs and code to call search_in_list function grouped.
       fuu fla;fha" break m%ldYhla fkdue;' break fjkqjg" fuh m%;sodk w.hka ,nd .ekSug return
       Ndú;d lrhs' main Y%s;h ;=<" wdodk ,nd .ekSug fla; iy search_in_list Y%s;h f.kaùug
       fla;h iuQy.; lr we;'

       # Using in operator
       def search_in_list(L, K):
          return K in L

       def main():
          tempL = input('Enter the list of numbers: ').split(',')
          L = [int(i) for i in tempL]
          K = int(input('Enter the value to be searched: '))
          print(search_in_list(L, K))

       main()

       In this code, for loop is replaced by built-in in operator. in operator works as a sequential searching
       algorithm.
       fuu fla;fha for Æmh" built-in fufyhjkhla jk in fufyhjkh uÕska m%;sia:dmkh lr we;'
       in fufyhjkh wkql%ñl fijqï we,af.dß;uhla f,i l%shd lrhs'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                               -106-                                          #ictfromabc

       # Using recursive function
       def search_in_list_recursive(L, K):
          if not L:
                return False
          if L[0] == K:
                return True
          return search_in_list_recursive(L[1:], K)

       def main():
          tempL = input('Enter the list of numbers: ').split(',')
          L = [int(i) for i in tempL]
          K = int(input('Enter the value to be searched: '))
          print(search_in_list_recursive(L, K))

       main()

       In this code, the code uses recursion to get the output. First if statement is to check if the list L is
       empty. The second if statements is to check the first element of the list is equal to K.
       fuu fla;fha" m%;sodkh ,nd .ekSu i|yd fla; mqkrdj¾;kh Ndú;d lrhs' m<uq if m%ldYh
       we;af;a L ,ehsia;=j ysia oehs mÍlaId lsÍughs' fojk if m%ldYh we;af;a ,ehsia;=fõ m<uq wx.h
       K g iudk oehs mÍlaId lsÍughs'

       Lastly, the return function is to slice the list by dropping first element every time it runs and repeat
       the instructions. This will run until the list becomes empty or there’s a match.
       wjidk jYfhka" return Y%s;h mqkrdj¾;kh jk iEu wjia:djlu m<uq wx.h y,d ouñka
       ,ehsia;=j fldgia lsÍu iy Wmfoia kej; l%shd;aul lsrSu i|yd fõ' ,ehsia;=j ysia jk ;=re fyda
       .e,mSula yuq jk ;=re fuh l%shd;aul fõ'

       # Using list comprehension
       def search_in_list(L, K):
          return True if [x for x in L if x == K] else False

       def main():
          tempL = input('Enter the list of numbers: ').split(',')
          L = [int(i) for i in tempL]
          K = int(input('Enter the value to be searched: '))
          print(search_in_list(L, K))

       main()

       In this code, the function used a shorthand method. It returns True or False based on the if statement.
       fuu fla;fha Y%s;h" flá fhÿï l%uhla Ndú;d lr we;' th if m%ldYh u; mokïj True fyda
       False wdmiq ,nd fohs'

       Inside the if statement, there’s a for loop and another if statement. This method is called list
       comprehension because,
       if m%ldYh ;=<" for Æmh iy ;j;a if m%ldYhla we;' fuu l%uh list comprehension f,i
       ye¢kafõ' ulaksido h;a"

       First, the for loop assigns a value to list x and then the if statement checks if x is equal to K. If it
       becomes true, it sends list x to the main if statement and since this is a singleton list and contains a
       value, the if becomes true and the return function will give 'True' as output.
       m<uqj" for Æmh x ,ehsia;=jg w.hla mjrk w;r miqj if m%ldYh x, K g iudk oehs mÍlaId
       lrhs' th i;H njg m;a jqjfyd;a" th list x m%Odk if m%ldYhg hjk w;r yd fuh ;ks
       wx.hla we;s ,ehsia;=jla jk neúka" if i;H jk w;r return Y%s;h m%;sodkh f,i 'True' ,nd
       fokq we;'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                              -107-                                          #ictfromabc

       If x is not equal to any of the values inside L, then x sends to the main if statement will be an empty
       list. Hence, the return function will output 'False'.
       x, L ;=< we;s lsisÿ w.hlg iudk fkdfõ kï" x m%Odk if m%ldYhg hjk w;r th ysia
       ,ehsia;=jla jkq we;' tneúka" return Y%s;h 'False' f,i m%;sodkh lrhs'

       The main if statement won’t get any inputs unless there’s a match or the for loop ends.
       .e,mSula fkdue;s kï fyda for Æmh wjika jk ;=re m%Odk if m%ldYhg lsisÿ wdodkhla
       fkd,efí'

       Why x is a list? / x ,ehsia;=jla jkafka wehs@

       In list comprehension, there’s a standard syntax. / list comprehension ;=<" iïu; syntax tlla fõ'

       if [x for x in iterable if condition]

       x – variable name / úp,H kduh

       iterable – list, range, string or the object you need to iterate on.
       ,ehsia;=j" range, string fyda Tng mqkrdj¾;kh ùug wjYH jia;=j'

       condition – The condition for inner if statement. / we;=<; if m%ldYh i|yd fldkafoaish'

       There are syntaxes for set, dictionary comprehension like this.
       fï wdldrhgu set, dictionary comprehension i|yd syntax we;'

       Example 1 / WodyrKh 1
       L = [22, 33, 56, 58, 78, 98, 11]
       K = 78

        Turn    x    x == K     [x for x in L if x == K]             return
        1       22   False      []                                   -
        2       33   False      []                                   -
        3       56   False      []                                   -
        4       58   False      []                                   -
        5       78   True       [78]                                 True

       The function returns True because 78 is found in the list.
       78 ,ehsia;=fõ we;s ksid Y%s;h True ,ndfohs'

       Example 2 / WodyrKh 2
       L = [22, 33, 56, 58, 78, 98, 11]
       K = [22, 33, 56, 58, 78, 98, 11]

        Turn    x    x == K [x for x in L if x == K] return

        1      22    False      []                                  -
        2      33    False      []                                  -
        3      56    False      []                                  -
        4      58    False      []                                  -
        5      78    False      []                                  -
        6      98    False      []                                  -
        7      11    False      []                                  False

       The function returns False because 12 is not found in the list.
       12 ,ehsia;=fõ ke;s ksid Y%s;h False ,ndfohs'

     (a) What is the cardinality of the relationship between the entites STUDENT and CLASS, denoted
         by (A) and (B) above? Note: Write down suitable labels for (A) and (B), respectively.
         (A) iy (B) f,i olajd we;s CLASS iy STUDENT hk N=;d¾: w;r iïnkaO;dfjys
         .KkSh;djh (cardinality) l=ula o? igyk: (A) iy (B) i|yd iqÿiq f,an, ms<sfj<ska ,shd

        Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu(
        Each class can have many students enrolled in it. For example, both students with Index number as
        8991 and 8995 are in the same class, which is 12-B (ClassID 1112).
        iEu mka;shlgu fndfyda isiqka we;=<;a lr .; yelsh' WodyrKhla f,i" 8991 iy 8995 f,i
        o¾Yl wxl we;s isiqka fofokdu 12-B (ClassID 1112) hk tlu mka;sfha isá;s'

        However, each student belongs to a single class. Here, IndexNumber in STUDENT table is unique in
        each record and one record only consists of one ClassID. Also, a student record cannot exist without
        referencing a valid ClassID.
        flfia fj;;a" iEu isiqfjl=u tla mka;shlg wh;a fõ' fuys§" STUDENT j.=fõ IndexNumber
        iEu Wm,elshdkhlu wkkH jk w;r tla Wm,elshdkhl wvx.= jkafka tla ClassID tlla muKs'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                            -109-                                        #ictfromabc

       In the STUDENT table, ClassID is repeated for multiple students who are in the same class. This
       confirms the “many” side (STUDENT) is tied to a single “one” side (CLASS).
       STUDENT j.=fõ" tlu mka;sfha isák nyq isiqka i|yd ClassID mqkrdj¾;kh fõ' fuh “nyq”
       me;a; (STUDENT), ;ks “tAl” me;a;g (CLASS) ne£ we;s nj ;yjqre lrhs'

       Therefore, this is a one-to-many relationship. / tneúka" fuh tal-nyq iïnkaO;djhls'

    (b) Explain how a relationship is established between the two tables using primary key(s) and foreign
        key(s) in the above example.
        by; WodyrKfhys m%d:ñl h;=r$h;=re iy wd.ka;=l h;=r$h;=re Ndú;fhka j.= fol w;r
        iïnkaO;djhla (relationship) f.dvkef.k wdldrh meyeos,s lrkak'

       ClassID is the primary key in CLASS table. IndexNumber is the primary key in STUDENT table. As
       in the given tables, ClassID is also a separate field in STUDENT table. Therefore, ClassID, which is
       the primary key of CLASS table is used as the foreign key in STUDENT table.
       CLASS j.=fõ m%d:ñl h;=r ClassID fõ. STUDENT j.=fõ m%d:ñl h;=r IndexNumber fõ.
       j.=j, § we;s wdldrhg wkqj" ClassID hkq STUDENT j.=fõ o we;s fjku lafIa;%hls' tneúka"
       CLASS j.=fõ m%d:ñl h;=r jk ClassID" STUDENT j.=fõ wd.ka;=l h;=r f,i Ndú;d lrhs'

    (c)(i) Are the two tables STUDENT and CLASS, in second normal form (2NF)? Explain a reason
           for your answer referring to tables.
           STUDENT iy CLASS j.= fojk m%u; (2NF) wdldrfhka mj;S o@ Tfí ms<s;=rg fya;=jla j.=
           wdY%fhka meyeÈ,s lrkak'
         Yes. / Tõ'

         Both STUDENT and CLASS tables are in 1NF as all fields are atomic. (No repeating records)
         ish¨u lafIa;% mrudKql neúka STUDENT iy CLASS j.= folu 1NF ys we;'
         (mqkrdj¾;kh jk Wm,elshdk fkdue;)

         Primary key is not composite in both tables. Only ClassID is the primary key in CLASS table and
         only IndexNumber is the primary key in STUDENT table. Hence, there are no partial
         dependencies. Therefore, the tables are in 2NF.
         j.= foflysu m%d:ñl h;=r ixhqla; fkdfõ' CLASS j.=fõ m%d:ñl h;=r ClassID muKla jk
         w;r STUDENT j.=fõ m%d:ñl h;=r IndexNumber muKs' tneúka" w¾O mrdh;a;;d fkdue;'
         tneúka" j.= 2NF ys we;'

         Every non-prime attribute of each relation is fully functional dependent on the primary key.
         Therefore, they are in 3NF as no transitive dependencies exists.
         iEu iïnkaO;djhlu iEu m%d:ñl fkdjk Wm,laIKhlau m%d:ñl h;=r u; iïmQ¾Kfhkau
         r|d mj;S' tneúka" lsisÿ ixl%dka;s mrdh;a;;djhla fkdmj;sk neúka tAjd 3NF ys we;'

         Therefore, both tables are already in 2NF. / tneúka" j.= folu oekgu;a 2NF ys we;'

     (ii) Briefly explain one key advantage of normalisation.
          m%u;lrKfhys m%Odk jdishla fláfhka meyeÈ,s lrkak'

         Advantages of normalization in databases / o;a; iuqodhka m%u;lrKfha jdis:

         1. Data integrity and consistency / o;a; wLKav;dj iy wkql+,;dj
         • Data integrity is maintained, reducing update, delete, and insert anomalies.
             hdj;ald,Sk lsÍu" uld oeóu iy we;=<;a lsÍfï úIu;d wvq lrñka o;a; wLKav;dj mj;ajd
             f.k hkq ,efí'

         •   The database is ACID compliant (Atomicity, Consistency, Isolation, Durability), ensuring
             reliable transactions.
             o;a; iuqodh ACID wkql+, fõ ^mrudKql;dj" wkql+,;dj" fjkaj mej;Su" l,ameje;au&"
             tuÕska úYajdiodhl .kqfokq iy;sl lrhs'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                             -110-                                          #ictfromabc

        2. Elimination of data redundancy and duplication
        o;a; w;sßla;h iy wkqmsgm;a lsÍu bj;a lsÍu

        •   No data duplication, preventing multiple copies of the same data.
            o;a; wkqmsgm;a lsÍula fkdue;s w;r tlu o;a;j, nyq msgm;a j<lajhs'
        •   A smaller size database is achieved by eliminating redundant data.
            w;sßla; o;a; bj;a lsÍfuka l=vd m%udKfha o;a; iuqodhla ,nd .; yelsh'

        3. Improved performance and faster access
        jeäÈhqKq l< ld¾hidOkh iy fõ.j;a m%fõYh

        •   Faster access speed, as there are fewer indexes per table.
            j.=jlg we;s o¾Yl .Kk wvq neúka" m%fõYh fõ.j;a fõ'

        •   Searching, sorting, and creating indexes is faster, as tables are narrower. Also, more rows fit
            on a data page.
            j.= mgq neúka o¾Yl fiùu" j¾. lsÍu iy ks¾udKh lsÍu fõ.j;a fõ' tfiau" tla
            o;a; msgqjlg jeä fma<s .Kkla .e,msh yelsh'

        •   Updates run quickly, as no data is duplicated in multiple locations.
            ia:dk lsysmhl lsisÿ o;a;hla wkqmsgm;a lr fkdue;s neúka hdj;ald,Sk lsÍï blaukska
            l%shd;aul fõ'

        •   Data inserts run quickly, since there is a single insertion point for each piece of data, avoiding
            duplication.
            o;a; we;=¿ lsÍï blaukska l%shd;aul fõ' ukao" tla tla o;a;h i|yd ;ks we;=<;a lsÍfï
            ,laIHhla we;s neúka" wkqmsgm;a lsÍu j<lajhs'

        •   Tables are usually smaller, allowing them to fit into the buffer, which improves database
            performance.
            j.= idudkHfhka l=vd jk w;r" tAjd buffer tlg .e<mSug bv ie,iSu uÕska o;a; iuqod
            ld¾h idOkh jeä ÈhqKq fõ'

        4. Ease of Maintenance and Modification / kv;a;= lsÍfï iy fjkia lsÍfï myiqj
        •   Conceptually cleaner and easier to maintain and change database structure.
            ixl,amuh jYfhka ksrjq,a iy kv;a;= lsÍug yd o;a; iuqod jHqyh fjkia lsÍug myiqh'

   (b)(i) Briefly explain the key difference between functional and non-functional requirements as used in
          the system development life cycle.

          •   Functional requirements : Any requirement which specifies what the system should do or provide
              for users/ they related to· the technical functionality of the system. function is described as a
              specification of behavior· between outputs and inputs/ behavior (output) that a device or software
              isexpected to exhibit in the case of a certain input/ desired operations of a software, or system.

              msßú;rhla f,i úia;r flf¾'

          •   Non-functional requirements : Any requirement which specifies how the system performs a
              certain function or system works. They describe how, how well or to what standard a function
              should be provided /nonfunctional requirements describe the general characteristics of a system.
              They are also known as quality attributes. (Include service hours, service availability,
              responsiveness, throughput and reliability.)/define system attributes such as security, reliability,
              performance, maintainability, scalability, and usability.
